# Physics — Questions

Answers: [answers.md](answers.md). Question numbers match answer numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1: Thermal physics

#### Temperature and the kinetic particle model

**Q1.** A patient has a body temperature of 37 °C. What is this temperature on the kelvin scale?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-1 -->

- A. 37 K
- B. 100 K
- C. 236 K
- D. 310 K

**Q2.** According to the kinetic particle model, the temperature of a substance is a measure of the
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-2 -->

- A. total energy stored in all the bonds between its particles
- B. average random kinetic energy of its particles
- C. number of particles in the sample
- D. total internal energy of the sample

**Q3.** A sealed, rigid container of gas is heated. Which statement best explains why the gas pressure increases?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-3 -->

- A. The particles repel each other more strongly
- B. The number of particles in the container increases
- C. The particles become larger and take up more space
- D. The particles move faster, so they hit the walls more often and with greater force

**Q4.** Container X holds 1 kg of water at 20 °C and container Y holds 2 kg of water at 20 °C. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-4 -->

- A. Y has a higher temperature than X
- B. X and Y have the same internal energy
- C. X has greater internal energy because its particles move faster
- D. X and Y have the same temperature but Y has greater internal energy

**Q5.** The absolute temperature of a sample of helium gas is increased from 300 K to 1200 K. The average translational kinetic energy of the atoms becomes
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-5 -->

- A. 2 times larger and the average speed 2 times larger
- B. 2 times larger and the average speed 4 times larger
- C. 4 times larger and the average speed 2 times larger
- D. 4 times larger and the average speed 4 times larger

**Q6.** Liquid nitrogen boils at 77 K. (a) Convert 77 K to °C. (b) Convert 20 °C to K. (c) Calculate the temperature difference between them in kelvin. (3 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q7.** Distinguish between the temperature and the internal energy of a body, using the kinetic particle model. Use an example comparing two bodies in your answer. (3 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-7 -->

*Written answer*

**Q8.** A rigid sealed can contains air. The can is placed in a hot oven. Use the kinetic particle model to explain why the pressure inside the can increases. (3 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-8 -->

*Written answer*

**Q9.** What is the freezing point of water, 0 °C, on the kelvin scale?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-9 -->

- A. 0 K
- B. 100 K
- C. 273 K
- D. −273 K

**Q10.** Absolute zero, 0 K, is closest to which temperature on the Celsius scale?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-10 -->

- A. −373 °C
- B. −273 °C
- C. 0 °C
- D. 273 °C

**Q11.** The temperature of a metal block rises by 25 K. What is the change in its temperature on the Celsius scale?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-11 -->

- A. 25 °C
- B. 298 °C
- C. −248 °C
- D. 248 °C

**Q12.** Tiny pollen grains suspended in water are observed under a microscope to jiggle in random directions. According to the kinetic particle model, this occurs because the grains are
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-12 -->

- A. attracted to the walls of the container
- B. repelled by each other because they carry like charges
- C. pushed by convection currents caused by the microscope lamp
- D. bombarded unevenly by fast-moving, randomly moving water molecules

**Q13.** A sample of hydrogen gas (molecular mass 2 u) and a sample of oxygen gas (molecular mass 32 u) are both at 300 K. Compared with oxygen molecules, the hydrogen molecules have
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-13 -->

- A. the same average kinetic energy but a greater average speed
- B. a greater average kinetic energy and a greater average speed
- C. the same average kinetic energy and the same average speed
- D. a smaller average kinetic energy but a greater average speed

**Q14.** In the kinetic particle model, absolute zero is the temperature at which the particles of a substance
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-14 -->

- A. are all at rest relative to each other and exert no forces
- B. have the greatest possible potential energy
- C. have the minimum possible random kinetic energy
- D. change from a liquid to a solid

**Q15.** The temperature of a gas is raised from 27 °C to 327 °C. The average translational kinetic energy of its particles
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-15 -->

- A. increases by a factor of about 12
- B. increases by 300 J
- C. remains the same
- D. doubles

**Q16.** Two objects are placed in contact and, after some time, there is no net transfer of energy between them. According to the kinetic particle model, the objects now have the same
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-16 -->

- A. average random kinetic energy per particle
- B. total internal energy
- C. number of particles
- D. average particle speed, even if the particle masses differ

**Q17.** A large bath of water at 30 °C is in thermal contact with a small cup of water at 80 °C. The net transfer of energy is
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-17 -->

- A. from the bath to the cup, because the bath has more internal energy
- B. zero, because both contain the same substance
- C. from the cup to the bath, because the cup has the higher temperature
- D. from the bath to the cup, because the bath has more particles

**Q18.** A gas is trapped in a sealed syringe at constant temperature. The plunger is pushed in so that the volume is halved. The pressure of the gas increases because
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-18 -->

- A. the particles move faster
- B. the particles hit the walls more often, since they have less distance to travel between collisions
- C. the particles become heavier
- D. each collision with the wall exerts a greater force

**Q19.** Which of the following is an assumption of the kinetic particle model of an ideal gas?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-19 -->

- A. The particles attract each other strongly at all separations
- B. The particles collide with the walls and each other so that they lose energy each time
- C. The volume of the particles is much greater than the volume of the container
- D. There are negligible forces between the particles except during collisions

**Q20.** A solid block is heated and its temperature rises while it remains solid. Which change best describes the particles?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-20 -->

- A. They vibrate about their fixed positions with a greater average kinetic energy
- B. They begin to move freely throughout the whole block
- C. They become more tightly packed
- D. They break away from the block and move through the air

**Q21.** A liquid-in-glass thermometer is calibrated by marking the liquid level at two fixed points. On the Celsius scale these fixed points are
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-21 -->

- A. −273 °C and 0 °C
- B. 0 °C at the freezing point of water and 273 °C at absolute zero
- C. 0 °C at the freezing point and 100 °C at the boiling point of water at standard atmospheric pressure
- D. 37 °C at body temperature and 100 °C at the boiling point of water

**Q22.** Why does the gas in a sealed container exert the same pressure on every wall, if the container is at a uniform temperature?
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-22 -->

- A. Gravity pulls the particles equally in every direction
- B. The particles move randomly in all directions, so on average equal numbers collide with each wall
- C. The particles only move in straight lines between the walls
- D. The walls are made of the same material

**Q23.** A sealed sample of gas is at 20 °C. It is heated until the average kinetic energy of its particles has doubled. The final temperature is closest to
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-23 -->

- A. 40 °C
- B. 313 °C
- C. 586 °C
- D. 1 K

**Q24.** The temperature of a gas rises from 300 K to 900 K. The average speed of its particles increases by a factor of approximately
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-24 -->

- A. 9
- B. 3
- C. 1.7
- D. 1.2

**Q25.** A block of ice at 0 °C is heated and begins to melt. While it is melting, the temperature stays at 0 °C. According to the kinetic particle model, the energy supplied is
<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-25 -->

- A. converted into an increase in the average kinetic energy of the particles
- B. lost to the surroundings so the particles slow down
- C. used to make the particles more massive
- D. used to increase the potential energy of the particles as the bonds between them are loosened

**Q26.** (a) Convert 25 °C to kelvin. (b) Convert 350 K to degrees Celsius. (2 marks)

**Q29.** Use the kinetic particle model to explain what is meant by two objects in thermal contact reaching thermal equilibrium. (3 marks)

<!-- id: physics12a1-tkp-31 -->

**Q32.** When a hot cup of water cools by evaporation, the temperature of the water that remains falls. Use the kinetic particle model to explain why. (3 marks)

**Q33.** A student states that raising the temperature of a gas from 10 °C to 20 °C doubles the average kinetic energy of its particles. Evaluate this claim with a calculation. (3 marks)

**Q34.** A gas is heated from 27 °C to 327 °C. The average kinetic energy of its particles:
<!-- id: vce-physics12-tk-c1 -->
*Challenge question*

- B. Doubles
- C. Stays the same

#### Specific heat capacity

**Q35.** How much energy is needed to raise the temperature of 0.500 kg of water from 20 °C to 60 °C? (c of water = 4180 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹)
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-1 -->

- A. 8.36 × 10⁴ J
- B. 1.25 × 10⁵ J
- C. 1.67 × 10⁵ J
- D. 4.18 × 10⁴ J

**Q36.** Equal masses of metal X (c = 900 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹) and metal Y (c = 380 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹) absorb the same amount of energy. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-2 -->

- A. Neither temperature changes
- B. X undergoes the greater temperature rise
- C. Y undergoes the greater temperature rise
- D. Both undergo the same temperature rise

**Q37.** The specific heat capacity of ice is 2100 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹. This means that
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-3 -->

- A. 2100 J is needed to raise the temperature of 1 kg of ice by 1 K
- B. 1 kg of ice at 0 °C contains 2100 J of energy
- C. ice must be at 2100 K to melt
- D. 2100 J is needed to melt 1 kg of ice

**Q38.** A 1500 W immersion heater, with no energy losses, heats 2.0 kg of water for 100 s. The temperature rise of the water is closest to (c of water = 4180 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹)
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-4 -->

- A. 36 K
- B. 72 K
- C. 9.0 K
- D. 18 K

**Q39.** Coastal cities usually have a smaller daily temperature range than inland cities. This is mainly because water
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-5 -->

- A. has a high specific heat capacity, so its temperature changes little for a large energy change
- B. has a low specific heat capacity, so it heats up quickly
- C. is a good conductor of heat
- D. reflects all incoming radiation

**Q40.** A 0.250 kg aluminium block at 100.0 °C is placed in 0.400 kg of water at 20.0 °C in an insulated container. Calculate the final equilibrium temperature. Use c(aluminium) = 900 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹ and c(water) = 4180 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹. (4 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q41.** A 2000 W electric kettle heats 1.50 kg of water from 18.0 °C to 100 °C. (a) Calculate the energy absorbed by the water. (b) Calculate the time taken if the kettle is 100% efficient. (c) Calculate the time taken if only 85% of the electrical energy is transferred to the water. (4 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-7 -->

*Written answer*

**Q42.** In an experiment, a 50 W heater is placed in a 1.0 kg metal block for 300 s and the block temperature rises by 17 K. (a) Calculate the value of the specific heat capacity of the metal that a student would obtain from these data. (b) The true value is lower than the student's value. Explain why, and suggest one improvement. (3 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-8 -->

*Written answer*

**Q43.** Which of the following is the SI unit of specific heat capacity?
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-9 -->

- A. J K⁻¹
- B. J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹
- C. J kg K⁻¹
- D. kg J⁻¹ K

**Q44.** How much energy is needed to raise the temperature of 2.0 kg of copper from 25 °C to 75 °C? (c of copper = 385 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹)
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-10 -->

- A. 1.93 × 10⁴ J
- B. 5.78 × 10⁴ J
- C. 3.85 × 10⁴ J
- D. 7.70 × 10⁴ J

**Q45.** An oil of specific heat capacity 2000 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹ absorbs 3.0 × 10⁴ J. If the mass of oil is 0.50 kg, its temperature rise is
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-11 -->

- A. 15 K
- B. 60 K
- C. 3.0 K
- D. 30 K

**Q46.** A graph of energy supplied Q (vertical axis) against temperature change ΔT (horizontal axis) is plotted for a sample of a substance. The gradient of the line equals
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-12 -->

- A. mc, the mass multiplied by the specific heat capacity
- B. c/m
- C. 1/(mc)
- D. m/c

**Q47.** 2.0 kg of water at 30 °C is mixed with 1.0 kg of water at 60 °C in an insulated container. The final temperature is
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-13 -->

- A. 45 °C
- B. 40 °C
- C. 50 °C
- D. 35 °C

**Q48.** 1.0 kg of water and 1.0 kg of copper are each heated through 10 K. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-14 -->

- A. The copper requires more energy because it conducts heat better
- B. The water requires less energy because it is a liquid
- C. The water requires more energy because it has the greater specific heat capacity
- D. Both require the same energy because the masses and temperature changes are equal

**Q49.** 0.20 kg of copper cools from 150 °C to 50 °C. The energy released is (c of copper = 385 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹)
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-15 -->

- A. 1.16 × 10⁴ J
- B. 3.85 × 10³ J
- C. 7.7 × 10⁴ J
- D. 7.7 × 10³ J

**Q50.** Water is commonly used as the coolant in car engines mainly because it
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-16 -->

- A. can absorb a large amount of energy for each kelvin of temperature rise
- B. has a very low boiling point
- C. has a lower specific heat capacity than metals
- D. does not expand when heated

**Q51.** Two samples of equal mass are heated by identical heaters and their temperatures are recorded against time. Sample P warms more quickly than sample Q. This shows that
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-17 -->

- A. P has a higher specific heat capacity than Q
- B. P has a lower specific heat capacity than Q
- C. P and Q have the same specific heat capacity
- D. P has a higher melting point than Q

**Q52.** A 0.50 kg metal sample absorbs 9.0 × 10³ J and its temperature rises by 40 K. Its specific heat capacity is
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-18 -->

- A. 225 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹
- B. 900 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹
- C. 180 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹
- D. 450 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹

**Q53.** In an experiment to find the specific heat capacity of a liquid heated by an electric heater, the liquid is stirred continuously. The main reason is to
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-19 -->

- A. make the temperature uniform so the thermometer reading represents the whole liquid
- B. increase the specific heat capacity of the liquid
- C. reduce the mass of liquid being heated
- D. remove the need to measure the heating time

**Q54.** 1.0 kg of water at 20 °C and 1.0 kg of aluminium at 100 °C are placed in contact in an insulated container. The final temperature will be
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-20 -->

- A. exactly 60 °C, because the masses are equal
- B. closer to 20 °C than to 100 °C, because water has the greater specific heat capacity
- C. closer to 100 °C than to 20 °C, because aluminium is hotter
- D. closer to 20 °C than to 100 °C, because water has the lower specific heat capacity

**Q55.** On a sunny day the sand on a beach becomes much hotter than the sea beside it. The best explanation is that sand
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-21 -->

- A. has a higher specific heat capacity than water
- B. absorbs more solar energy than water in every case
- C. cannot lose energy to the air
- D. has a lower specific heat capacity than water, so the same energy produces a larger temperature rise

**Q56.** Samples A and B are both made of the same metal. A has mass 1.0 kg and B has mass 2.0 kg. The same amount of energy is supplied to each. The ratio ΔT(A) : ΔT(B) is
<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-22 -->

- A. 2 : 1
- B. 1 : 2
- C. 1 : 1
- D. 4 : 1

<!-- id: physics12a1-shc-24 -->

#### Latent heat and phase change

**Q66.** How much energy is needed to melt 0.200 kg of ice at 0 °C? (latent heat of fusion of water = 3.34 × 10⁵ J kg⁻¹)
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-1 -->

- A. 6.68 × 10⁴ J
- B. 3.34 × 10⁵ J
- C. 1.67 × 10⁶ J
- D. 4.52 × 10⁵ J

**Q67.** Water is boiling at a steady 100 °C on a stove. According to the kinetic particle model, the energy supplied is being used to
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-2 -->

- A. reduce the mass of the water
- B. increase the average kinetic energy of the particles
- C. increase the potential energy of the particles as the bonds between them are overcome
- D. decrease the number of particles

**Q68.** Steam at 100 °C causes a more severe burn than the same mass of water at 100 °C because
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-3 -->

- A. steam releases a large amount of latent heat as it condenses on the skin
- B. steam has a lower specific heat capacity than water
- C. steam conducts heat better than water
- D. steam has a higher temperature

**Q69.** 1 kg of ice at −10 °C is heated at a constant rate until it is steam at 110 °C. Which stage of the process takes the longest time? (c ice = 2100, c water = 4180 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹; L fusion = 3.34 × 10⁵, L vaporisation = 2.26 × 10⁶ J kg⁻¹)
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-4 -->

- A. warming the water from 0 °C to 100 °C
- B. boiling the water to steam
- C. warming the ice to 0 °C
- D. melting the ice

**Q70.** How much energy is required to change 0.100 kg of ice at 0 °C to water at 20 °C? (L fusion = 3.34 × 10⁵ J kg⁻¹; c water = 4180 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹)
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-5 -->

- A. 8.36 × 10³ J
- B. 3.34 × 10⁴ J
- C. 4.18 × 10⁴ J
- D. 4.18 × 10⁵ J

*Written answer*

<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-13 -->

- A. changes of state, where energy goes into potential energy and temperature is constant

<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-14 -->

<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-15 -->

- A. 1.05 × 10⁵ J
- B. 5.65 × 10⁵ J
- C. 8.35 × 10⁴ J

**Q81.** Evaporation of sweat cools the skin. According to the particle model, this is because
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-16 -->

- A. the sweat particles gain energy from the air and become colder
- B. evaporation increases the mass of the sweat
- C. evaporating particles stop moving

**Q82.** A 500 W heater supplies all its energy to 0.100 kg of ice at 0 °C. How long does it take to melt all the ice? (L fusion = 3.34 × 10⁵ J kg⁻¹)
<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-17 -->

- A. 66.8 s
- B. 452 s

<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-18 -->

- A. 6.68 × 10³ J
- B. 4.52 × 10⁴ J
- C. 8.36 × 10³ J
- D. 5.36 × 10⁴ J

<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-19 -->

- A. stays the same because the temperature is constant
- B. decreases because the kinetic energy of the particles decreases

<!-- id: physics12a1-lat-20 -->

- A. a higher specific heat capacity than the liquid
- B. a lower latent heat than the liquid
- C. a higher mass than the liquid

#### Nuclear stability and isotopes

#### Half-life and activity

#### Nuclear fission and fusion

**Q377.** Which statement best describes nuclear fission?
<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-1 -->

- D. A nucleus emits an alpha particle spontaneously

<!-- id: physics12a2-nff-2 -->

#### Electrical safety

**Q431.** What is the main safety function of the earth wire in a mains-powered appliance with a metal casing?
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-1 -->

- A. It increases the resistance of the appliance to reduce power use.
- B. It provides a low-resistance path to earth so that a fault current becomes large enough to blow the fuse or trip the circuit breaker.
- C. It limits the normal operating current of the appliance.
- D. It supplies the return path for the normal operating current.

**Q432.** A 230 V appliance has a power rating of 1.15 kW. Which fuse rating is most appropriate to protect it?
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-2 -->

- A. 5 A
- B. 30 A
- C. 10 A
- D. 2 A

**Q433.** A residual current device (RCD) disconnects the supply when it detects
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-3 -->

- A. any increase in the frequency of the supply.
- B. a current in the live wire above 10 A.
- C. a voltage drop across the neutral wire.
- D. a difference between the current in the live and neutral wires of a few tens of milliamperes.

**Q434.** Why does a double-insulated appliance not require an earth wire?
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-4 -->

- A. Its plug has two pins, which makes it safer.
- B. Its casing and all user-accessible parts are non-conducting, so the live wire cannot make them live even if an internal fault occurs.
- C. It operates at a lower voltage than other appliances.
- D. The neutral wire acts as the earth.

**Q435.** Which statement best explains the danger of electric shock to a person?
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-5 -->

- A. Direct current is always more dangerous than alternating current.
- B. The danger depends on the size of the current through the body, the path it takes and how long it flows.
- C. The danger depends only on the supply voltage and not on the current.
- D. A shock is dangerous only if it lasts longer than one hour.

**Q436.** Explain how a fuse protects a household appliance circuit, and why the fuse is placed in the live wire rather than the neutral wire. (3 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q437.** A toaster with a metal casing is connected to the mains through a three-pin plug. A fault causes the live wire inside the toaster to touch the casing. Describe what happens if (a) the casing is connected to the earth wire, and (b) the toaster is not earthed. (3 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-7 -->

*Written answer*

**Q438.** A person with wet hands, with a body resistance of 1500 Ω, touches a live conductor at 230 V and a good earth. (a) Calculate the current through the person. (b) Explain why an RCD with a 30 mA trip threshold would protect the person but a 15 A circuit breaker would not. (3 marks)
<!-- id: physics12a3-es-8 -->

*Written answer*

**Q439.** A residual current device (RCD) protects people by:
<!-- id: vce-physics12-es-c1 -->
*Challenge question*

- A. Melting a thin wire when the current becomes too high for the wiring in the circuit
- B. Connecting the neutral wire to earth so that any fault current flows safely away
- C. Detecting a difference between the currents in the active and neutral wires, then cutting the power
- D. Lowering the supply voltage whenever someone touches an exposed live part

<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-4 -->

- B. They increase the impulse, so the car stops more quickly.
- C. They make the collision perfectly elastic.
- D. They increase the time over which the car stops, reducing the average force for the same change in momentum.

**Q471.** A 1.0 kg trolley moving at 4.0 m s⁻¹ collides with an identical stationary trolley. Afterwards, the first moves at 1.0 m s⁻¹ and the second at 3.0 m s⁻¹, both in the original direction. The collision is best described as
<!-- id: physics12a3-mi-5 -->

- A. inelastic, because momentum is conserved but kinetic energy is not.
- B. elastic, because momentum is conserved.
- C. elastic, because the trolleys separate afterwards.
- D. inelastic, because momentum is not conserved.

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**Q504.** An electric kettle
<!-- id: vce-physics12-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

An electric kettle is rated at 2000 W when connected to the 230 V mains supply.

**a.** Calculate the current through the kettle and the resistance of its heating element. *(2 marks)*

**b.** The kettle heats 1.50 kg of water from 20.0 °C to 100.0 °C. Calculate the energy absorbed by the water. The specific heat capacity of water is 4200 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Calculate the minimum time the kettle takes to do this, assuming all the electrical energy heats the water. *(2 marks)*

The kettle is left on, and 0.100 kg of the water boils away. The specific latent heat of vaporisation of water is 2.26 × 10⁶ J kg⁻¹.

**d.i.** Calculate the energy needed to boil away this water. *(1 mark)*

**d.ii.** Explain why the temperature of the water stays at 100 °C while it boils, even though energy is still being supplied. *(1 mark)*

**e.** Name the main way heat spreads through the water from the element at the bottom of the kettle. *(1 mark)*

**f.** The kettle has a metal case connected to the earth wire. Explain how the earth wire, together with a fuse or circuit breaker, protects the user if the live wire touches the case. *(2 marks)*

**Q505.** A cyclist on the road
<!-- id: vce-physics12-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

A cyclist and bicycle have a combined mass of 80 kg. Starting from rest, they accelerate uniformly to 10 m s⁻¹ in 5.0 s along a flat road. Use g = 9.8 m s⁻².

**a.** Calculate the acceleration of the cyclist. *(1 mark)*

**b.** Calculate the distance travelled in the 5.0 s. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Friction and air resistance together exert 40 N against the motion. Calculate the driving force the cyclist must provide during this acceleration. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Calculate the kinetic energy of the cyclist and bicycle at 10 m s⁻¹. *(1 mark)*

**e.** The cyclist then free-wheels down a hill, dropping 5.0 m in height. Ignoring friction and air resistance, calculate the speed at the bottom if the speed at the top is 10 m s⁻¹. *(2 marks)*

**f.** At the bottom, the cyclist brakes and slows from 14 m s⁻¹ to rest in 2.0 s. Calculate the magnitude of the impulse on the cyclist and bicycle, and the average braking force. *(2 marks)*

**g.** Use Newton's first law to explain why the cyclist's body tends to move forward when the bicycle stops suddenly. *(1 mark)*

**Q506.** Radiation in medicine
<!-- id: vce-physics12-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Technetium-99m is a gamma emitter with a half-life of 6.0 hours. A patient is injected with a dose with an activity of 800 MBq.

**a.** Calculate the activity remaining after 24 hours. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Explain why a gamma emitter with a short half-life is suitable for medical imaging. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Which type of radiation is the most ionising and the least penetrating? *(1 mark)*

**d.** A patient of mass 70 kg absorbs 0.050 J of gamma radiation. Calculate the absorbed dose and the equivalent dose (gamma weighting factor 1). *(2 marks)*

**e.** Cobalt-60 undergoes beta-minus decay to nickel. Write the nuclear equation. *(2 marks)*

**f.** One fission of uranium-235 releases about 200 MeV. Convert this energy to joules. *(1 mark)*

**Q507.** Light bending and splitting
<!-- id: vce-physics12-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Light travels from air (refractive index 1.00) into glass (refractive index 1.50) at an angle of incidence of 40°.

**a.** Calculate the angle of refraction. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Calculate the speed of light in the glass. *(1 mark)*

**c.** Calculate the critical angle for light passing from this glass into air. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Explain how optical fibres use total internal reflection to carry light. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Explain why white light splits into colours in a glass prism. *(2 marks)*

**f.** Calculate the wavelength of a 100 MHz radio wave. *(1 mark)*

**Q508.** Challenge: a kettle, a fuse and the power bill
<!-- id: vce-physics12-cx1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Multi-part question · 11 marks · Original challenge question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

An electric kettle is rated at 2400 W when connected to a 240 V supply. It transfers 90% of its electrical energy to the water. Use c(water) = 4200 J/kg/K and L(vaporisation) = 2.26 × 10⁶ J/kg.

**a.** Calculate the current in the kettle and its resistance. *(2 marks)*

**b.** How long does the kettle take to heat 1.5 kg of water from 20 °C to 100 °C? *(3 marks)*

**c.** The kettle's switch fails, and 0.10 kg of water boils away. How much longer did the kettle run? *(2 marks)*

**d.** Which fuse should protect the kettle's circuit: 3 A, 5 A, 13 A or 30 A? Explain. *(2 marks)*

**e.** The kettle is used for 4 minutes, 6 times a day, for 30 days. At $0.30 per kWh, how much does it cost? *(2 marks)*

**Q509.** Challenge: braking and collisions
<!-- id: vce-physics12-cx2 -->
*Challenge question*
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original challenge question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

In a test, a 1200 kg car travelling at 20 m/s brakes uniformly and stops in 4.0 s.

**a.** Find the car's acceleration and the braking force. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Find the braking distance, and the total stopping distance if the driver's reaction time is 0.75 s. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Show that the work done by the brakes equals the car's initial kinetic energy. *(2 marks)*

In a second test, the car moves at 10 m/s and hits a stationary 800 kg car. The two cars lock together.

**d.** Find their speed just after the collision. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Explain, using impulse, how a crumple zone reduces the force on passengers. *(2 marks)*

#### Newton's laws and forces

- C. 0 N

### Special relativity

#### Einstein's postulates and frames

**Q822.** Which statement is Einstein's first postulate of special relativity?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-1 -->

- A. The laws of physics are the same in all inertial frames of reference.
- B. The speed of light depends on the motion of its source.
- C. Time passes at the same rate for all observers.
- D. All motion is measured relative to a stationary aether.

**Q823.** Which statement is Einstein's second postulate of special relativity?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-2 -->

- A. The speed of light is greatest when the source moves towards the observer.
- B. The speed of light in a vacuum is the same for all observers, regardless of the motion of the source or observer.
- C. Light requires a medium, the aether, to travel through.
- D. Nothing can travel at the speed of light, including light pulses in a vacuum.

**Q824.** An inertial frame of reference is best described as a frame that is:
<!-- id: physics-4-1-3 -->

- A. moving at the speed of light
- B. accelerating at a constant rate
- C. not accelerating, so it is at rest or moving at constant velocity
- D. attached to the surface of the Sun

**Q825.** Which of the following is NOT an inertial frame of reference?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-4 -->

- A. A V/Line train travelling at a constant 120 km/h on a straight, level track
- B. A spacecraft drifting with its engines off, far from any planets or stars
- C. A laboratory bench at rest on Earth's surface (to a good approximation)
- D. A car travelling around a roundabout at a constant 40 km/h

**Q826.** A spacecraft travelling at 0.50c relative to Earth switches on a forward-pointing headlight. What speed does an observer on Earth measure for the light from the headlight?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-5 -->

- A. c
- B. 1.5c
- C. 0.50c
- D. 1.25c

**Q827.** A spacecraft moves away from Earth at 0.60c. A laser pulse is sent from Earth towards the spacecraft. What speed does the astronaut on the spacecraft measure for the pulse?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-6 -->

- A. 0.40c
- B. c
- C. 1.6c
- D. 0.60c

**Q828.** What was the main result of the Michelson–Morley experiment?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-7 -->

- A. A clear fringe shift showed Earth's speed through the aether.
- B. Light was found to travel faster in the direction of Earth's motion.
- C. No significant change in the interference pattern was detected, giving no evidence for the aether.
- D. Light was shown to need a medium to travel through space.

**Q829.** A passenger in a windowless carriage of a smoothly moving train at constant velocity drops a ball and does several pendulum experiments. Which idea explains why she cannot work out the train's speed from these experiments?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-8 -->

- A. Light travels at the same speed for all observers.
- B. Objects with mass cannot reach the speed of light.
- C. Time dilation makes moving clocks run slow.
- D. The laws of physics are the same in all inertial frames.

**Q830.** Proper time is the time interval between two events measured by an observer:
<!-- id: physics-4-1-9 -->

- A. for whom the two events occur at the same location
- B. who is at rest on Earth
- C. who is moving fastest relative to the events
- D. who measures the longest time interval

**Q831.** The proper length of an object is the length measured by an observer who is:
<!-- id: physics-4-1-10 -->

- A. moving at the speed of light relative to the object
- B. at rest relative to the object
- C. moving at the greatest possible speed relative to the object
- D. on Earth, regardless of the object's motion

**Q832.** Two lightning strikes hit opposite ends of a long platform at the same time according to an observer standing on the platform. According to a passenger on a train moving rapidly along the platform, the strikes:
<!-- id: physics-4-1-11 -->

- A. must occur at the same time, because simultaneity is absolute
- B. occur at the same time only if the train is accelerating
- C. generally do not occur at the same time
- D. occur at the same location

**Q833.** Einstein was motivated to develop special relativity mainly by a conflict between:
<!-- id: physics-4-1-12 -->

- A. Newton's law of gravitation and Kepler's laws
- B. the wave and particle models of electrons
- C. Ohm's law and Faraday's law
- D. Maxwell's electromagnetism, which predicts a fixed speed of light, and Galilean (classical) velocity addition

**Q834.** Which quantity is measured to have the same value by all inertial observers, whatever their relative motion?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-13 -->

- A. The speed of light in a vacuum
- B. The time interval between two events
- C. The length of a moving rod
- D. The kinetic energy of a moving particle

**Q835.** A train moves at speed v relative to the track and turns on its headlight. Using classical Galilean velocity addition, what speed would a trackside observer be predicted to measure for the light?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-14 -->

- A. c
- B. c + v
- C. c − v
- D. v

**Q836.** Which statement about the scope of special relativity is correct?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-15 -->

- A. It applies only to objects travelling at the speed of light.
- B. It applies only to accelerating frames of reference.
- C. It applies to observers in inertial (non-accelerating) frames of reference.
- D. It applies only to light and not to objects with mass.

**Q837.** Why can a spacecraft with mass never reach the speed of light?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-16 -->

- A. Air resistance in space becomes too large near c.
- B. The spacecraft's length would become negative.
- C. The second postulate forbids objects from moving faster than 0.5c.
- D. As v approaches c, the Lorentz factor approaches infinity, so infinite energy would be needed.

**Q838.** Lightning strikes the front and back of a fast-moving train simultaneously in the ground frame. A passenger sits exactly in the middle of the train. According to the passenger, which strike happened first?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-17 -->

- A. The strike at the front of the train
- B. The strike at the back of the train
- C. Both strikes happened at the same time
- D. It depends on the colour of the light

**Q839.** Astronauts Ana and Ben pass each other in spacecraft moving at constant relative velocity. Each finds that the other's clock is running slow. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: physics-4-1-18 -->

- A. Only Ana is correct, because she noticed it first.
- B. Both are correct, because each is in an inertial frame and the situation is symmetric.
- C. Only the faster astronaut is correct.
- D. Neither is correct, because clocks always run at the same rate.

- D. Light behaves as a wave in a vacuum and as a particle in matter.

### Practical investigations

#### Experimental design and variables

**Q1238.** A student investigates how the length of a pendulum affects its period. What is the independent variable?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-1 -->

- A. The length of the pendulum
- B. The period of the pendulum
- C. The mass of the pendulum bob
- D. The amplitude of the swing

**Q1239.** In an investigation of how the length of a pendulum affects its period, what is the dependent variable?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-2 -->

- A. The length of the string
- B. The period of oscillation
- C. The number of swings timed
- D. The gravitational field strength

**Q1240.** A student investigates how the voltage across a resistor affects the current through it. Which is a controlled variable?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-3 -->

- A. The current through the resistor
- B. The voltage across the resistor
- C. The temperature of the resistor
- D. The ammeter reading

**Q1241.** Why must controlled variables be kept constant during an experiment?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-4 -->

- A. To increase the resolution of the measuring instruments
- B. To eliminate all random errors from the results
- C. To guarantee that the hypothesis is supported
- D. So that any change in the dependent variable can be attributed to the independent variable

**Q1242.** Which is the best hypothesis for an investigation of how drop height affects the bounce height of a tennis ball?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-5 -->

- A. If the drop height increases, then the bounce height will increase proportionally, because a constant fraction of the ball's energy is lost in each bounce
- B. The tennis ball will bounce when it is dropped
- C. Bounce height depends on many different factors
- D. Drop height is the independent variable and bounce height is the dependent variable

**Q1243.** In VCE Physics, what does the term 'validity' of an investigation refer to?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-6 -->

- A. The closeness of agreement between repeated measurements
- B. The extent to which the experiment actually investigates what it claims to investigate
- C. The closeness of a measurement to the true value
- D. The smallest change in a quantity that an instrument can detect

**Q1244.** Which statement best describes 'repeatability'?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-7 -->

- A. The closeness of agreement between results obtained by different people using different equipment
- B. The closeness of a mean value to the accepted value
- C. The closeness of agreement between successive measurements made by the same person, with the same method and equipment, under the same conditions
- D. The number of significant figures given by an instrument

**Q1245.** A Year 12 class in Ballarat obtains a value for g that closely agrees with a value obtained by a class in Geelong using different equipment. This agreement demonstrates good:
<!-- id: physics-8-1-8 -->

- A. repeatability
- B. resolution
- C. validity of the controlled variables
- D. reproducibility

**Q1246.** A student investigates how the mass of a stack of paper coffee filters affects their terminal velocity. Which action best improves the validity of the investigation?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-9 -->

- A. Dropping every stack from the same height in the same draught-free room
- B. Using only the heaviest stacks to make timing easier
- C. Recording times to more decimal places than the stopwatch displays
- D. Changing both the number of filters and their shape between trials

**Q1247.** Between trials, a student changes both the length of a pendulum and the mass of its bob. What is the main problem with this design?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-10 -->

- A. The period cannot be measured if the mass changes
- B. Two variables have been changed, so a change in period cannot be attributed to either one
- C. Changing the mass always introduces a systematic error
- D. The experiment will become more precise but less accurate

**Q1248.** Which is the most appropriate risk control when using a laser to investigate double-slit interference?
<!-- id: physics-8-1-11 -->

- A. Wear ordinary sunglasses so the beam can be viewed directly
- B. Aim the beam at shiny surfaces to make the pattern brighter
- C. Use a low-power (Class 2) laser and never look directly into the beam or its reflections
- D. Use the most powerful laser available to make the fringes clearer

**Q1249.** Which of the following is a qualitative observation?

- A. The current was 2.4 A
- B. The temperature rose by 3.2 °C
- C. The wire was 45.0 cm long
- D. The wire became noticeably warm to the touch

**Q1250.** A student measures the period of a pendulum for lengths of 0.40 m, 0.42 m and 0.44 m only. Which change would best improve the design?

**Q1251.** Why do students usually time 10 complete oscillations of a pendulum rather than a single oscillation?

#### Accuracy, precision and uncertainty

**Q1264.** In VCE Physics, what does 'accuracy' describe?
<!-- id: physics-8-2-1 -->

- A. How close a measurement is to the true value
- B. How close repeated measurements are to each other
- C. The smallest division on the measuring scale
- D. The number of significant figures recorded

**Q1265.** In VCE Physics, what does 'precision' describe?
<!-- id: physics-8-2-2 -->

- A. How close a measurement is to the true value
- B. How closely a set of repeated measurements agree with each other
- C. The size of the systematic error
- D. The range of the independent variable

- B. both accurate and precise
- C. precise but not accurate

#### Random and systematic errors

**Q1290.** Which statement best describes random error?
<!-- id: physics-8-3-1 -->

- A. Unpredictable variations that scatter readings either side of the true value
- B. A consistent offset that shifts every reading in the same direction
- C. An error caused by misreading a scale once
- D. An error that can be removed by calibrating the instrument

**Q1291.** Which statement best describes systematic error?
<!-- id: physics-8-3-2 -->

- A. An error that scatters readings randomly above and below the true value
- B. An error that shifts every reading in the same direction by the same amount or proportion
- C. An error that is removed by taking more readings
- D. An error that only affects the precision of results

**Q1292.** An electronic balance reads 0.3 g with nothing on the pan. This is an example of:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-3 -->

- A. a random error
- B. a mistake
- C. a systematic (zero) error
- D. a resolution limit

**Q1293.** A student's reaction time varies slightly each time they start and stop a stopwatch. This mainly introduces:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-4 -->

- A. systematic error
- B. a zero error
- C. a calibration error
- D. random error

**Q1294.** Averaging many repeated measurements reduces the effect of:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-5 -->

- A. random errors only
- B. systematic errors only
- C. both random and systematic errors equally
- D. neither random nor systematic errors

**Q1295.** A student records a length of 25 cm when the scale actually read 2.5 cm. According to VCE Physics terminology, this is best described as:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-6 -->

- A. a random error that is reduced by averaging
- B. a mistake (personal error) that should be avoided or the reading repeated, not treated as an experimental error
- C. a systematic error caused by the instrument
- D. an uncertainty due to the instrument's resolution

**Q1296.** A student always reads a ruler from the same angle above the pointer, so every reading is shifted the same way. This is:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-7 -->

- A. a random error due to parallax
- B. a resolution error
- C. a systematic error due to parallax
- D. an outlier

**Q1297.** The first 2 mm of a metre rule has been worn away, and a student measures lengths starting from the worn end. What is the effect?
<!-- id: physics-8-3-8 -->

- A. Every reading is 2 mm too small, a systematic error
- B. Readings are randomly 2 mm too large or too small
- C. There is no effect because the scale is still marked in millimetres
- D. Every reading is 2 mm too large, a systematic error

**Q1298.** A graph of extension against force for a spring is expected to pass through the origin but has a non-zero intercept. This most likely indicates:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-9 -->

- A. a systematic error
- B. a random error
- C. that the spring obeys Hooke's law exactly
- D. that more data points are needed to reduce precision

**Q1299.** One measurement in a set of repeated trials is far from the others. What is the most appropriate response?
<!-- id: physics-8-3-10 -->

- A. Always include it so the data are not manipulated
- B. Check for a mistake, repeat the measurement if possible, and exclude it from the mean with a note in the report if it is clearly anomalous
- C. Delete it without comment
- D. Change it to match the mean of the other values

**Q1300.** Which action reduces systematic error?
<!-- id: physics-8-3-11 -->

- A. Taking more repeated readings
- B. Using a larger range of the independent variable
- C. Calibrating the instrument against a known standard
- D. Recording more decimal places

**Q1301.** In a free-fall experiment, air resistance on a light ball causes the measured g to be:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-12 -->

- A. consistently higher than the true value, a systematic error
- B. randomly higher or lower, a random error
- C. unaffected because air resistance is negligible for all objects
- D. consistently lower than the true value, a systematic error

**Q1302.** The reading on a voltmeter fluctuates slightly up and down because of electrical noise in the circuit. This is:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-13 -->

- A. random error
- B. systematic error
- C. a zero error
- D. a mistake

**Q1303.** A stopwatch runs 2% fast, so every time it records is 2% too long. Which statement about the results is correct?
<!-- id: physics-8-3-14 -->

- A. The results will be imprecise but accurate
- B. The results may still be precise but they will be inaccurate
- C. The results will be both accurate and precise
- D. Averaging more trials will remove the error

**Q1304.** Increasing the number of trials from 3 to 10 will most likely:
<!-- id: physics-8-3-15 -->

- A. remove all systematic errors
- B. make each individual reading more accurate
- C. reduce the effect of random errors on the mean, but not change any systematic error
- D. increase the resolution of the instrument

**Q1305.** A thermometer reads 1.5 °C too high at all temperatures. It is used to find the temperature change of water in a specific heat capacity experiment. What is the effect on the calculated temperature change?
<!-- id: physics-8-3-16 -->

- A. The temperature change will be 1.5 °C too high
- B. The temperature change will be 3.0 °C too high
- C. The temperature change will be 1.5 °C too low
- D. No effect, because the offset cancels when the initial temperature is subtracted from the final

**Q1306.** A light gate calculates speed as card length ÷ time. The card length is entered as 5.0 cm when it is actually 4.0 cm. What is the effect on the calculated speeds?
<!-- id: physics-8-3-17 -->

- A. All speeds are 25% too high, a systematic error
- B. All speeds are 20% too low, a systematic error
- C. Speeds are randomly too high or too low
- D. There is no effect because time is measured accurately

- A. Random errors mainly affect accuracy; systematic errors mainly affect precision
- B. Random errors mainly affect precision; systematic errors mainly affect accuracy
- C. Both mainly affect resolution
- D. Neither affects accuracy

#### Graphing and linearisation

**Q1316.** When graphing experimental data, which variable is conventionally plotted on the horizontal (x) axis?
<!-- id: physics-8-4-1 -->

- A. The independent variable
- B. The dependent variable
- C. The controlled variable
- D. The variable with the larger uncertainty

**Q1317.** The period of a pendulum is given by T = 2π√(L/g). Which graph will give a straight line through the origin?
<!-- id: physics-8-4-2 -->

- A. T against L
- B. T² against L
- C. T against L²
- D. √T against L

**Q1318.** A graph of T² against L for a pendulum has a gradient of 4.0 s²/m. What is the value of g?
<!-- id: physics-8-4-3 -->

- A. 2.5 m/s²
- B. 0.10 m/s²
- C. 9.9 m/s²
- D. 39 m/s²

**Q1319.** The intensity I of light from a point source is thought to follow I = k/r². Which graph should be plotted to test this?
<!-- id: physics-8-4-4 -->

- A. I against r²
- B. I against r
- C. I against 1/r
- D. I against 1/r²

**Q1320.** For an object in uniform circular motion with fixed mass and radius, F = mv²/r. Which graph gives a straight line, and what is its gradient?
<!-- id: physics-8-4-5 -->

- A. F against v², gradient m/r
- B. F against v, gradient m/r
- C. F against v², gradient r/m
- D. F against 1/v², gradient mr

**Q1321.** A graph of current (y-axis) against voltage (x-axis) for a resistor has a gradient of 0.040 A/V. What is the resistance?
<!-- id: physics-8-4-6 -->

- A. 0.040 Ω
- B. 25 Ω
- C. 4.0 Ω
- D. 2.5 Ω

**Q1322.** Which best describes a line of best fit for linear data?
<!-- id: physics-8-4-7 -->

- A. A line joining each data point to the next
- B. A line that must pass through the first and last points
- C. A straight line with points scattered roughly evenly on either side, not necessarily passing through every point or the origin
- D. A line that must always pass through the origin

**Q1323.** Theory predicts that a graph should pass through the origin, but the line of best fit has a clear non-zero intercept. This most likely indicates:
<!-- id: physics-8-4-8 -->

- A. only random errors in the measurements
- B. that the theory has been proven correct
- C. that the gradient is zero
- D. a systematic error in the measurements

**Q1324.** For the photoelectric effect, a graph of maximum kinetic energy of photoelectrons against frequency of light is a straight line. What does the x-intercept represent?

- A. The threshold frequency
- B. Planck's constant
- C. The work function
- D. The stopping voltage

**Q1325.** A trolley accelerates uniformly from rest. A graph of displacement s against t² has a gradient of 1.5 m/s². What is the acceleration?

- B. 3.0 m/s²
- C. 0.75 m/s²
- D. 2.25 m/s²

- A. λ against p

- D. λ against p²

**Q1327.** What does the area under a force–extension graph for a spring represent?
<!-- id: physics-8-4-12 -->

- A. The spring constant
- B. The momentum of the spring
- C. The power delivered to the spring

**Q1328.** What is the purpose of error bars on a graph?

- B. To show the line of best fit

**Q1329.** Students draw the steepest and least steep lines that still pass through all the error bars on a graph. What is this used for?

### Challenge questions

#### Challenge set

**Q1342.** Astronauts in a space station 400 km above Earth feel weightless because:

- D. They are outside Earth's gravitational field, so only the Moon and the Sun pull on them, weakly

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**Q1352.** Satellites in orbit
<!-- id: physics-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks · Original question in the style of the VCE Physics exam, Section B*

A satellite moves in a circular orbit 600 km above Earth's surface. Use G = 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ N m² kg⁻², mass of Earth = 5.98 × 10²⁴ kg and radius of Earth = 6.37 × 10⁶ m.

**a.** Calculate the gravitational field strength at the satellite's orbit. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Calculate the orbital speed of the satellite. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Calculate the period of the orbit, in minutes. *(2 marks)*

**d.** An astronaut inside the satellite feels weightless, even though the gravitational field strength there is not zero. Explain why. *(2 marks)*

A geostationary satellite orbits Earth once every 24.0 hours.

**e.** Use Kepler's third law to calculate the orbital radius of the geostationary satellite. *(2 marks)*

The satellites send data to the ground using radio waves of frequency 1.50 GHz.

**f.** Calculate the wavelength of these radio waves. Use c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹. *(1 mark)*

**Q1353.** Electrons: accelerated, turned and diffracted
<!-- id: physics-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 13 marks · Original question in the style of the VCE Physics exam, Section B*

In an electron gun, electrons start from rest and are accelerated between two parallel plates 2.0 cm apart, with a potential difference of 2.00 kV between them. Use e = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ C, mₑ = 9.1 × 10⁻³¹ kg, h = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ J s and c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹. Ignore relativistic effects.

**a.** Calculate the magnitude of the electric field between the plates, and the magnitude of the force it exerts on an electron. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Calculate the kinetic energy of an electron as it leaves the plates, in joules. *(1 mark)*

**c.** Show that the speed of the electrons is about 2.65 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹. *(2 marks)*

The electrons then enter a uniform magnetic field of 5.00 mT, directed at right angles to their velocity.

**d.** Calculate the radius of the electrons' circular path in the magnetic field. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Explain why the magnetic field does not change the speed of the electrons. *(2 marks)*

Electrons with this speed are then aimed at a thin crystal, where they produce a diffraction pattern.

**f.i.** Calculate the de Broglie wavelength of the electrons. *(2 marks)*

**f.ii.** X-rays produce the same diffraction pattern from this crystal. Calculate the energy of one of these X-ray photons, in keV. *(2 marks)*

**Q1354.** From generator to town
<!-- id: physics-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of the VCE Physics exam, Section B*

A coil of 200 turns, each of area 0.050 m², sits in a uniform magnetic field of 0.40 T. It is rotated from a position where its plane is perpendicular to the field to a position where its plane is parallel to the field, in 0.025 s.

**a.** Calculate the change in magnetic flux through one turn of the coil. *(1 mark)*

**b.** Calculate the average EMF induced in the coil. *(2 marks)*

**c.** State Lenz's law as it applies to the current induced in the coil. *(1 mark)*

A power station supplies 250 kW to a town at a transmission voltage of 5.00 kV RMS. The transmission lines have a total resistance of 4.0 Ω.

**d.** Calculate the current in the transmission lines. *(1 mark)*

**e.** Calculate the power lost in the transmission lines. *(2 marks)*

**f.** The voltage is stepped up to 50.0 kV before transmission. Calculate the new power loss, and explain why it is so much smaller. *(2 marks)*

**g.** Calculate the peak voltage corresponding to 5.00 kV RMS. *(1 mark)*

**Q1355.** Green light: waves and photons
<!-- id: physics-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of the VCE Physics exam, Section B*

Green light of wavelength 532 nm passes through two narrow slits 0.250 mm apart and forms an interference pattern on a screen 2.00 m away.

**a.** Calculate the distance between adjacent bright fringes on the screen. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Explain why a dark fringe forms at some points on the screen. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Describe the effect on the fringe spacing of using red light instead. *(1 mark)*

The same light shines on a metal surface with a work function of 2.10 eV. Use h = 4.14 × 10⁻¹⁵ eV s and c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹.

**d.** Calculate the energy of one photon of this light, in eV. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Calculate the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted photoelectrons. *(1 mark)*

**f.** The intensity of the light is doubled. Explain the effect on the number of photoelectrons and on their maximum kinetic energy. *(2 marks)*

**Q1356.** Challenge: electrons as particles and waves
<!-- id: physics-cx1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Multi-part question · 11 marks · Original challenge question in the style of the VCE Physics exam, Section B*

In an electron gun, electrons are accelerated from rest through a potential difference of 2000 V. They then enter a region of uniform magnetic field of 2.0 mT, perpendicular to their velocity.

**a.** Calculate the kinetic energy of each electron in joules. *(1 mark)*

**b.** Calculate the speed of the electrons. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Justify the use of non-relativistic equations for these electrons. *(1 mark)*

**d.** Calculate the radius of the electrons' circular path in the magnetic field. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Explain why the electrons' speed does not change in the magnetic field. *(1 mark)*

**f.** Calculate the de Broglie wavelength of the electrons. *(2 marks)*

**g.** X-rays with the same wavelength pass through the same crystal. Compare the diffraction patterns and the energy of an X-ray photon with that of an electron. *(2 marks)*

**Q1357.** Challenge: from wind turbine to town
<!-- id: physics-cx2 -->
*Challenge question*
*Multi-part question · 11 marks · Original challenge question in the style of the VCE Physics exam, Section B*

A simple generator has a 100-turn coil of area 0.020 m² rotating at 50 revolutions per second in a uniform 0.40 T magnetic field.

**a.** Calculate the average emf induced as the coil turns from parallel to the field to perpendicular to it (a quarter turn). *(2 marks)*

**b.** State the effect on the peak emf and the frequency of doubling the rotation speed. *(2 marks)*

A wind farm produces 2.0 MW at 690 V RMS. A step-up transformer raises this to 66 kV for a transmission line of total resistance 8.0 Ω.

**c.** Find the turns ratio (primary : secondary) of the ideal step-up transformer. *(1 mark)*

**d.** Calculate the current in the line, the power lost in it and the voltage drop along it. *(3 marks)*

**e.** Show that sending the power at 690 V directly would not work. *(2 marks)*

**f.** Explain why a transformer cannot work with the DC output of a battery. *(1 mark)*
